Designing secure PUF-based authentication protocols for constrained environments
Authors: Sang-Woong Lee, Masoumeh Safkhani, Quynh Le, Omed Hassan Ahmed, Mehdi Hosseinzadeh, Amir Masoud Rahmani, Nasour Bagheri
Published: 2023

ABSTRACT
Physical Unclonable Functions (PUFs) are widely used in cryptographic authentication and key-agreement protocols due to their unique physical properties. This article presents a comprehensive cryptanalysis of two recently developed authentication protocols, namely PLAKE and EV-PUF, both relying on PUFs. Our analysis reveals significant vulnerabilities in these protocols, including susceptibility to impersonation and key leakage attacks, which pose serious threats to the security of the underlying systems. In the case of PLAKE, we propose an attack that can extract the shared secret key with negligible complexity by eavesdropping on consecutive protocol sessions. Similarly, we demonstrate an efficient attack against EV-PUF that enables the determination of the shared key between specific entities. Furthermore, we highlight the potential for a single compromised client in the EV-PUF protocol to compromise the security of the entire network, leaving it vulnerable to pandemic attacks. These findings underscore the critical importance of careful design and rigorous evaluation when developing PUF-based authentication protocols. To address the identified vulnerabilities, we present an improved PUF-based authentication protocol that ensures robust security against all the attacks described in the context of PLAKE and EV-PUF. Through this research, we contribute to the field by exposing vulnerabilities in existing PUF-based authentication protocols and offering an improved protocol that enhances security and safeguards against various attack vectors. This work serves as a valuable reference for researchers and practitioners involved in the design and implementation of secure authentication schemes for IoT systems and dynamic charging systems for electric vehicles.

Introduction

The Internet of Things (IoT) has profoundly transformed our interaction with the environment, encompassing domains such as smart homes and industrial automation. Nevertheless, the widespread adoption of IoT devices has introduced novel security challenges, necessitating the development of lightweight and secure authentication protocols1. Conventional cryptographic solutions tend to be excessively resource-intensive and costly for IoT devices, which typically operate under resource constraints. Consequently, there is an increasing demand for alternative solutions that offer robust security2, while simultaneously being lightweight and cost-effective3.

Secure communication systems require authentication as a crucial component to ensure that only authorized users or processes can access sensitive information or resources. Authentication protocols typically involve the exchange of credentials, such as usernames and passwords, to verify the identity of the user or process4. However, recent research has exposed vulnerabilities in many authentication protocols that attackers can exploit by exploiting weaknesses in the protocol design or implementation5,6. Conducting such security analyses aims to contribute to the broader understanding and knowledge of the design of secure protocols. It is important to subject any security mechanism to thorough independent analysis before placing trust in its effectiveness, for instance.

PUFs have emerged as a promising solution for enhancing the security of IoT devices7–9. PUFs are hardware-based primitives that generate a unique and unpredictable response to a challenge, which can serve as a secret key for authentication purposes. In comparison to traditional cryptographic solutions, PUFs offer lightweight, cost-effective, and IoT device-compatible features. Consequently, PUFs have garnered significant attention for the design of cryptographic authentication and key agreement protocols due to their distinctive physical properties, which make them highly resistant to tampering and cloning attacks10–15. These protocols find wide application in various domains, including Internet of Things (IoT) systems15 and electric vehicle (EV) charging stations16. However, the security of PUF-based protocols relies heavily on the design and implementation of said protocols.

In this paper, we conduct a cryptanalysis of two recent PUF-based authentication protocols: PLAKE15 and EV-PUF16. PLAKE is a mutual authentication protocol specifically designed for IoT systems, while EV-PUF is an authentication protocol tailored for dynamic charging systems of electric vehicles. Our analysis exposes vulnerabilities in both protocols, including susceptibility to impersonation attacks and key leakage attacks, which could compromise the security of the systems they aim to safeguard. These findings emphasize the significance of meticulous design and thorough evaluation of PUF-based authentication protocols to ensure their security. Furthermore, we present an improved protocol for enhancing the security of such applications.

Motivation

It is widely acknowledged that before any new security mechanism can be deemed trustworthy, its security must be thoroughly vetted by third-party experts to ensure it is not vulnerable to potential shortcomings. Two recently proposed authentication protocols, namely PLAKE15 and EV-PUF16, are notable for their reliance on physical unclonable functions (PUFs) as a foundational building block to provide robust security against a wide range of attacks, including cloning attacks. Additionally, both protocols leverage one-way hash functions to ensure data integrity. The designers of these schemes have claimed a high level of security, yet no independent security evaluations have been conducted to validate their assertions to the best of our knowledge. Given these shared characteristics, we are motivated to shed light on the security level of these protocols and determine whether they have indeed achieved their claimed level of security.

Our contribution

The primary contribution of this paper is a comprehensive security evaluation of two recently proposed authentication protocols that rely on PUFs, namely PLKE and EV-PUF.

Our analysis of PLKE reveals a novel attack that enables efficient recovery of secret parameters for this protocol. Specifically, our proposed attack allows an adversary to determine the shared key for all sessions after a minimum of two consecutive sessions, based solely on the messages transmitted over the public channel. The attack is passive and has negligible complexity, and can also be extended to enable impersonation and desynchronization attacks.

In addition, our security evaluation of EV-PUF exposes several vulnerabilities that undermine the security guarantees claimed by the protocol. Specifically, we demonstrate that the protocol does not provide forward secrecy and is susceptible to attacks by privileged insider adversaries, impersonation attacks, and session key compromise. Furthermore, we show that compromising a single node within the network can have a significant impact on the security of all other nodes, rendering the protocol vulnerable to pandemic attacks17. All of the proposed attacks are highly efficient and easy to execute.

The paper presents a novel PUF-based authentication protocol designed to provide secure authentication and key-agreement for IoT systems or electric vehicle charging systems. The protocol leverages the unique physical properties of PUFs combined with a sound but lightweight cipher suite (Ascon18) to establish mutual authentication and generate shared secret keys.

Paper orgnaization

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. Section “Preliminaries” provides an overview of PUFs and their applications in Internet of Things (IoT) security, as well as a description of target protocols. In this section, we also introduce adversary models. In Sections “Security analysis of PLAKE” and “Security analysis of EV-PUF”, we discuss, respectively, the security analysis of the PLAKE and EV-PUF protocols. In Sections “PUF-Based Mutual Authentication Protocol”, taking the lessons learned from our analysis, we propose a new PUF-based mutual authentication protocol and its security and cost analysis are given in Sections “PUF-Based Mutual Authentication Protocol”. Finally, we conclude the paper in Section “Conclusion”.

An overview on PUF

A Physical Unclonable Function (PUF) is a hardware security primitive that exploits the unique and unpredictable variations in physical characteristics of a device to generate a unique identifier or a cryptographic key. PUFs can be classified based on different criteria, such as their operating principle, the type of physical variations they exploit, and the type of output they produce19, 20.

One classification is based on the operating principle, where PUFs can be divided into two main categories: challenge-response PUFs and true random number generators (TRNGs) with PUF-based entropy sources. Challenge-response PUFs generate a response to a challenge input based on the unique physical characteristics of the device, while TRNGs with PUF-based entropy sources extract randomness from the physical variations in the device.

Another classification is based on the type of physical variation exploited by PUFs. PUFs can exploit various physical characteristics, such as delay, noise, power consumption, and ring oscillator frequencies, among others. For example, a ring oscillator (RO) PUF exploits the variations in the oscillation frequency of a ring oscillator circuit due to manufacturing process variations or environmental factors.

PUFs can also be classified based on the type of output they produce. PUFs can produce binary or multi-bit responses, depending on the application requirements. For example, a binary PUF produces a single-bit response, while a multi-bit PUF produces multiple bits of output.

Some commonly used PUFs include the Arbiter PUF, the RO PUF, and the Magnetic PUF, among others. The performance and security characteristics of PUFs depend on various factors, such as design parameters, the quality of the physical variations exploited, and environmental conditions.

PUFs have emerged as a promising solution for improving the security of devices in the Internet of Things (IoT) due to their low power consumption, small footprint, and resistance to physical and side-channel attacks21–23.

PUFs have found numerous applications in IoT security, including secure bootstrapping, secure firmware updates, secure key exchange, and secure communication. For example, PUFs can be used to generate unique device identities that are resistant to cloning and counterfeiting, allowing secure device authentication and access control23. PUF-based key exchange protocols can be used to establish secure communication channels between IoT devices24–28. Furthermore, recent research has focused on developing new PUF-based security mechanisms that address the limitations of traditional PUFs. For example, PUFs based on machine learning have been proposed that are more resistant to modeling attacks29. Recent surveys and reviews have provided a comprehensive analysis of the state of the art of PUFs, including their architectures, protocols, and security for Internet of Things (IoT) applications23,30. These publications have investigated the role of PUFs in IoT security, analyzed PUF-based threats on IoT devices, discussed possible defense strategies, and presented existing PUF architectures and authentication protocols using PUFs. They have also evaluated the progress, challenges, and future expectations of PUF-based security protocols for IoT devices23,30.

In summary, PUFs are a promising security primitive for IoT devices that can be used to enhance the security of authentication, key exchange, and data protection protocols. Recent research has shown increasing interest in PUF-based security mechanisms, and further exploration of their potential in IoT security is ongoing.

Adversary model

The security of the protocols is evaluated using two different adversary models: the adversary model “Dolev–Yao (DY)”31 and the “Canetti-Krawczyk (CK)”32 adversary model.

In the DY adversary model, the adversary is assumed to have complete control over the communication channels between parties but does not have access to their internal values. This means that the adversary can intercept, modify, or replay messages between the parties but cannot extract any secret information from them.

In contrast, the CK adversary model is a stronger adversary model that allows the adversary to extract secret credentials and compromise established session keys. This means that the adversary can obtain access to the internal values of the parties and use this information to compromise the security of the protocol.

By evaluating the security of the protocols under both adversary models, we can ensure whether they provide sufficient protection against attacks from both weaker and stronger adversaries.

Another attack model which is considered in this paper is the pandemic session key disclosure attack. Bagheri et al. introduced the concept of a pandemic attack17, which includes the pandemic session key disclosure attack. This attack occurs when an adversary has compromised a client \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_i\in \mathscr {C}$$\end{document}Ci∈C and is attempting to establish a session key with a different client \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_j\ne \mathscr {C}_i$$\end{document}Cj≠Ci.

PLAKE

Because of the rising amount of cyber assaults, the security of Internet of Things (IoT) systems has become a serious problem. Before transmitting sensitive data, mutual authentication confirms the identity of both the device and the server. Physical unclonable functions (PUFs) have emerged as a potential alternative to the mutual authentication of an IoT system. PUFs are hardware-based security primitives that provide unique and unpredictable answers to challenge input, allowing devices and services to be authenticated33. In this section, we briefly review the PLAKE15 which is a PUF-based mutual authentication protocol for IoT systems. The PLAKE protocol runs in two phases: a one-time enrollment phase and a device authentication and key exchange phase.

One-Time Enrollment (OTE) phase

EV-PUF

The Intelligent Transportation System (ITS) facilitates the communication between vehicles through Vehicular to Vehicular (V2V) and Vehicular to Infrastructure networks34. Wireless Power Transfer (WPT) technology has emerged as a promising solution to charge electric vehicles (EV) in ITS, allowing electric vehicles to charge their batteries while driving. Inductive power transfer technology (IPT) is a type of WPT that has been shown to be effective for EV charging35. For example, Sweden is currently building the world’s first permanent electrified road for EVs that utilizes an inductive charging system buried under the road surface to send electricity to a coil in the EV36. Electrified roadways, which integrate wireless charging infrastructure into asphalt, have the potential to enable EVs to operate continuously with unlimited power.

The EV-PUF protocol is an authentication protocol designed for the dynamic charging systems of electric vehicles, which utilizes a lightweight approach based on PUF technology. The protocol involves five key components, namely the Trusted Service Provider (TSP), Region Charging Server (RCS), Road Side Units (RSUs), Electric Vehicle (EV), Charging Pads (CP), and users (drivers). The proposed protocol includes many steps but we just explain those parts that are required to understand the proposed attacks. An interested reader could find all details of the EV-PUF in Ref.16. It should be noted EV-PUF uses two variants of PUF, i.e. RPUF, and WPUF. However, it has no effect on our attack, and the proposed attacks work for any type of PUF. Hence, for the sake of simplicity of notation, we just use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PUF(\cdot )$$\end{document}PUF(·) to describe both.

Initialization

In this phase of the protocol, the TSP selects a secure cryptographic hash function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}(\cdot )$$\end{document}H(·), private keys \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<SK_{RSU_i},SK_{TCS_i},SK_{EV}>$$\end{document}<SKRSUi,SKTCSi,SKEV>. It also selects a group key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{pad}$$\end{document}Gpad and forwards it to each RSU and each CP.

RSU registration

To register an RSU, the RSU generates a random identifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}IDRSUi and sends it to TSP. The TSP computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{RSU_i}=\mathscr {H}(ID_{RSU_i}\Vert SK_{RSU_i})$$\end{document}KRSUi=H(IDRSUi‖SKRSUi) and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}IDRSUi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}KRSUi in a table \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{RSU}$$\end{document}TRSU. Then it generates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PU_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}PURSUi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PR_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}PRRSUi respectively as the public and private key of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${RSU_i}$$\end{document}RSUi and stores them. Next, TSP sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$< PU_{RSU_i}, PR_{RSU_i}, K_{RSU_i},\mathscr {H}(SK_{RSU_i})>$$\end{document}<PURSUi,PRRSUi,KRSUi,H(SKRSUi)> to RSU.

RCS registration

To register an RCS, it generates a random identifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{RCS_i}$$\end{document}IDRCSi and sends it to TSP. The TSP computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{RCS_i}=\mathscr {H}(ID_{RCS_i}\Vert SK_{RCS_i})$$\end{document}KRCSi=H(IDRCSi‖SKRCSi) and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{RCS_i}$$\end{document}IDRCSi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{RCS_i}$$\end{document}KRCSi in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{RCS}$$\end{document}TRCS, as a table. Then generates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PU_{RCS_i}$$\end{document}PURCSi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PR_{RCS_i}$$\end{document}PRRCSi, respectively, as the public and private key of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${RCS_i}$$\end{document}RCSi and stores them. Finally, TSP, sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$< PU_{RCS_i}, PR_{RCS_i}, K_{RCS_i},\mathscr {H}(RCS_i)>$$\end{document}<PURCSi,PRRCSi,KRCSi,H(RCSi)> to RCS.

User registration

During the user registration, the user generates a random password \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PW_u$$\end{document}PWu and identifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_u$$\end{document}IDu and forwards them to TSP. The TSP computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=\mathscr {H}(PW_u\Vert ID_u)$$\end{document}i=H(PWu‖IDu), selects a secret P and a random number z, computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=\mathscr {H}(i\Vert P)$$\end{document}L=H(i‖P) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SID=\mathscr {H}(z)$$\end{document}SID=H(z) and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<ID_u,P,SID>$$\end{document}<IDu,P,SID> in a smart-card SC and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<L,P,SID>$$\end{document}<L,P,SID> in EV.

EV registration

To register an EV, it selects a random identifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{EV}$$\end{document}IDEV and a random number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{EV}$$\end{document}REV to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PID_{EV}=\mathscr {H}(ID_{EV}\Vert R_{EV})$$\end{document}PIDEV=H(IDEV‖REV) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{EV}=R_{EV}\oplus \mathscr {H}(ID_{EV})$$\end{document}YEV=REV⊕H(IDEV) and send \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<PID_{EV}>$$\end{document}<PIDEV> to TSP. The TSP computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_S=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert SK_{EV})$$\end{document}QS=H(PIDEV‖SKEV) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{EV}=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert W_S)$$\end{document}CEV=H(PIDEV‖WS). Next, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<ID_{RCS_i},ID_{RSU_i},R_S>$$\end{document}<IDRCSi,IDRSUi,RS> is stored in a table entitled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{EV}$$\end{document}TEV and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<PID_{EV},C_{EV},T_{R=1}>$$\end{document}<PIDEV,CEV,TR=1> is stored in a table entitled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{C}$$\end{document}TC. Then, it produces two challenges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i$$\end{document}Ci and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_x$$\end{document}Cx and sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<C_i,C_x, W_S,T_{EV}, \mathscr {H}(SK_{RSU_i})>$$\end{document}<Ci,Cx,WS,TEV,H(SKRSUi)> to EV. The EV computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_s=W_S\oplus PID_{EV}$$\end{document}Xs=WS⊕PIDEV and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{EV}=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert W_S)$$\end{document}CEV=H(PIDEV‖WS) and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_S$$\end{document}XS instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_S$$\end{document}WS. Then, it selects a random key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i$$\end{document}ri to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_x=PUF^{EV}(C_x)$$\end{document}Rx=PUFEV(Cx), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_i=\mathscr {H}(R_x\Vert r_i)$$\end{document}ki=H(Rx‖ri), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=C_i\oplus k_i$$\end{document}C=Ci⊕ki, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_i\oplus PUF(C)$$\end{document}Ri⊕PUF(C) and store \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<X_S,Y_{EV}, T_{EV}, C_{EV}, \mathscr {H}(SK_{RSU_i})>$$\end{document}<XS,YEV,TEV,CEV,H(SKRSUi)> in a tamper-proof memory and sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<PID_{EV}, R_i,K_i, R_x)>$$\end{document}<PIDEV,Ri,Ki,Rx)> to TSP. Once the message has been received, TSP stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<PID_{EV}, C_i, R_i,k_i, R_x>$$\end{document}<PIDEV,Ci,Ri,ki,Rx> in a table entitled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{CRP}$$\end{document}TCRP.

User authentication

In this phase of the protocol, the user / owner of the EV inserts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PW_u$$\end{document}PWu and given the timestamp \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_c$$\end{document}Tc, SC computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=\mathscr {H}(PW_u\Vert ID_u)$$\end{document}i=H(PWu‖IDu), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=\mathscr {H}(i\Vert P)$$\end{document}L=H(i‖P) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K=\mathscr {H}(L\Vert T_c\Vert SID)$$\end{document}K=H(L‖Tc‖SID) and sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<K\Vert T_c\Vert SID>$$\end{document}<K‖Tc‖SID> to EV. EV verifies whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_c-T_r<T$$\end{document}Tc-Tr<T and also the received K to authenticate the user and update SID as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SID^{new}=\mathscr {H}(SID\Vert P)$$\end{document}SIDnew=H(SID‖P) in its database and in the SC’s memory.

Login and mutual authentication

This phase of the protocol takes place between EV and RSU. On the EV side, the identifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{EV}$$\end{document}IDEV is inserted to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{EV}=Y_{EV}\oplus \mathscr {H}(ID_{EV})$$\end{document}REV=YEV⊕H(IDEV), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PID_{EV}=\mathscr {H}(ID_{EV}\Vert R_{EV})$$\end{document}PIDEV=H(IDEV‖REV) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_S=X_S\oplus PID_{EV}$$\end{document}WS=XS⊕PIDEV and verify whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{EV}=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert W_S)$$\end{document}CEV=H(PIDEV‖WS). Next, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_{RSU_i}$$\end{document}IDRSUi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_S$$\end{document}RS are taken from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{EV}$$\end{document}TEV to extract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_S=R_S\oplus PID_{EV}$$\end{document}QS=RS⊕PIDEV. Then, two random values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN_1$$\end{document}RN1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN_2$$\end{document}RN2 are generated to calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=PID_{EV}\oplus \mathscr {H}(ID_{RSU_i}\Vert n-1\Vert \mathscr {H}(SK_{RSU_i}))$$\end{document}B=PIDEV⊕H(IDRSUi‖n-1‖H(SKRSUi)), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert Q_S\Vert RN_1\Vert B)$$\end{document}D=H(PIDEV‖QS‖RN1‖B), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_x=PUF(C_x)$$\end{document}Rx=PUF(Cx), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^*_2=RN_2\oplus k_i$$\end{document}n2∗=RN2⊕ki, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1=\mathscr {H}(n^*_2\Vert k_i)$$\end{document}V1=H(n2∗‖ki). Then it sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<B,D,RN_1,n^*_2,V_1>$$\end{document}<B,D,RN1,n2∗,V1> toward RSU.

Once the message has been received, the RSU computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PID_{EV}=B\oplus \mathscr {H}(ID_{RSU_i}\Vert n-1\Vert \mathscr \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_S=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert K_{RSU_i})$$\end{document}QS=H(PIDEV‖KRSUi) and verifies whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=\mathscr {H}(PID_{EV}\Vert Q_S\Vert RN_1\Vert B)$$\end{document}D=H(PIDEV‖QS‖RN1‖B) accepts the request and achieves the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<C_i,R_i,k_i,R_x>$$\end{document}<Ci,Ri,ki,Rx> from the TSP, verifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1$$\end{document}V1, choosing a random number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN_3$$\end{document}RN3, computing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN_2=n^*_2\oplus k_i$$\end{document}RN2=n2∗⊕ki, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^*_3=RN_3\oplus k_i$$\end{document}n3∗=RN3⊕ki, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{1*}_i=R^1_i\oplus k_i$$\end{document}Ri1∗=Ri1⊕ki and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2=\mathscr {H}(n^*_3\Vert k_i\Vert R^{1*}_i\Vert RN_2)$$\end{document}V2=H(n3∗‖ki‖Ri1∗‖RN2). Then the RSU sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<C_i,R^{1*}_i,n^{*}_3,V_2>$$\end{document}<Ci,Ri1∗,n3∗,V2> to EV.

EV computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN_3=n^*_3\oplus k_i$$\end{document}RN3=n3∗⊕ki and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^1_i=R^{1*}_i\oplus k_i$$\end{document}Ri1=Ri1∗⊕ki to verify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2$$\end{document}V2 and authenticate RSU. Next, it computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=C_i\oplus k_i$$\end{document}C=Ci⊕ki, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{1+}_i\Vert R^{2+}_i=PUF(C)$$\end{document}Ri1+‖Ri2+=PUF(C) and verifies whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$FHD(R^{1+}_i,R^{1}_i)<\tau$$\end{document}FHD(Ri1+,Ri1)<τ. After mutual authentication with RSU, the authenticated EV can request a charge by sending \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<CH^i_{req},N_{ct}>$$\end{document}<CHreqi,Nct>. Once received the message, EV once again computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$KDF_{SK}(S_i)=K_1\Vert K_2$$\end{document}KDFSK(Si)=K1‖K2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_4=\mathscr {H}(K_1\Vert K_2\Vert N_ct)$$\end{document}V4=H(K1‖K2‖Nct) to verify the correctness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_4$$\end{document}V4. The first CP compares the Tag received from EV and the encrypted one from RSU.

Handover

The handover phase occurs if the EV moves from an RSU to another RSU or from an RCS to another RCS. In this regard, and when the EV moves from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Security analysis of PLAKE

In15, TABLE IV], the designers claimed the security of PLAKE against various attacks, including impersonation, replay, secret key extraction, denial of service and PUF-CRP extraction. In this section, we evaluate the security of PLAKE in an adversary model similar to that of the designers15, Sec. III.B], i.e. YD and CK models to shed light on the protocol security from an independent third-party point of view.

Secret disclosure attack

In the authentication phase described in Section “PLAKE”, the server generates two nonces, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$RN^i$$\end{document}RNi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$sk^i_{AB}$$\end{document}skABi. The server then computes several values, including \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^i_A=R^i_{A}\oplus RN^i$$\end{document}MAi=RAi⊕RNi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^i_B=R^i_{B}\oplus RN^i$$\end{document}MBi=RBi⊕RNi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^i_{AB}=sk^i_{AB}\oplus RN^i$$\end{document}YABi=skABi⊕RNi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^i_A=\mathscr {H}(R^i_{A}\Vert M^i_A)$$\end{document}HAi=H(RAi‖MAi), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^i_B=\mathscr {H}(R^i_{B}\Vert M^i_B)$$\end{document}HBi=H(RBi‖MBi). The server sends the message \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<C^i_{A}, M^i_A,M^i_B,H^i_A,H^i_B, Y^i_{AB}>$$\end{document}<CAi,MAi,MBi,HAi,HBi,YABi> to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and the message \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<C^i_{B}, M^i_A,M^i_B,H^i_A,H^i_B, Y^i_{AB}>$$\end{document}<CBi,MAi,MBi,HAi,HBi,YABi> to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB through a public channel. These values are used by the nodes to authenticate the server.

Once \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB have authenticated the server and each other, they proceed to the update phase, where they update their CRP responses. Specifically, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA updates its CRP response to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{i+1}_{A}$$\end{document}CAi+1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^i_{A}\oplus R^i_{A}$$\end{document}CAi⊕RAi and its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+1}_{A}$$\end{document}RAi+1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PUF^A(C^{i+1}_{A})$$\end{document}PUFA(CAi+1); similarly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB updates its CRP response to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{i+1}_{B}$$\end{document}CBi+1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^i_{B}\oplus R^i_{B}$$\end{document}CBi⊕RBi and its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+1}_{B}$$\end{document}RBi+1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PUF^B(C^{i+1}_{B})$$\end{document}PUFB(CBi+1). Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB respectively calculate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^i_{SA}= R^{i+1}_{A} \oplus RN^i$$\end{document}MSAi=RAi+1⊕RNi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^i_{SA}=\mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{A}\Vert M^i_{SA})$$\end{document}HSAi=H(RAi+1‖MSAi) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^i_{SB}= R^{i+1}_{B} \oplus RN^i$$\end{document}MSBi=RBi+1⊕RNi, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^i_{SB}=\mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{B}\Vert M^i_{SB})$$\end{document}HSBi=H(RBi+1‖MSBi), and respectively sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Now suppose that an adversary has eavesdropped on two consecutive sessions between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB, and the server, namely the i th and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+1){th}$$\end{document}(i+1)th sessions. In this case, the adversary has access to the information that was transmitted over the public channel during the i th session.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^i_{A}{} & {} \\ C^i_{B}{} & {} \\ M^i_A = &\; {} sk^i_{AB}\oplus R^i_{B}\\ Y^i_A = &\; {} sk^i_{AB}\oplus R^i_{A}\\ M^i_{SA} = &\; {} R^{i+1}_{A} \oplus RN^i\\ M^i_{SB} = &\; {} R^{i+1}_{B} \oplus RN^i\\ H^i_{SA} = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{A}\Vert M^i_{SB}) \\ H^i_{SB} = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{B}\Vert M^i_{SB}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CAiCBiMAi=RAi⊕RNiMBi=RBi⊕RNiYABi=skABi⊕RNiHAi=H(RAi‖MAi)HBi=H(RBi‖MBi)YBi=skABi⊕RBiYAi=skABi⊕RAiMSAi=RAi+1⊕RNiMSBi=RBi+1⊕RNiHSAi=H(RAi+1‖MSBi)HSBi=H(RBi+1‖MSBi)where,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^{i+1}_{A} = &\; {} C^i_{A}\oplus R^i_{A}\\ C^{i+1}_{B} = &\; {} C^i_{B}\oplus R^i_{B}\\ R^{i+1}_{A} = &\; {} PUF^A(C^{i+1}_{A})\\ R^{i+1}_{B} = &\; {} PUF^B(C^{i+1}_{B})\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CAi+1=CAi⊕RAiCBi+1=CBi⊕RBiRAi+1=PUFA(CAi+1)RBi+1=PUFB(CBi+1)It is important to note that the information that was transferred over the public channel during the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+1){th}$$\end{document}(i+1)th session may not be accessible to the adversary, as it was transmitted after the eavesdropping occurred. However, assuming that the adversary has access to the information that was transmitted during the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+1){th}$$\end{document}(i+1)th session because the attack was passive which increases the adversary’s chance, then it has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^{i+1}_{A}{} & {} \\ C^{i+1}_{B}{} & {} \\ M^{i+1}_A = &\; {} R^{i+1}_{A}\oplus RN^{i+1}\\ M^{i+1}_B = &\; {} R^{i+1}_{B}\oplus RN^{i+1}\\ T'^{i+1}_{AB} = &\; {} sk^{i+1}_{AB}\oplus RN^{i+1} \\ H^{i+1}_A = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{A}\Vert M^{i+1}_A)\\ H^{i+1}_B = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+1}_{B}\Vert M^{i+1}_B)\\ Y^{i+1}_B = &\; {} sk^{i+1}_{AB}\oplus R^{i+1}_{B}\\ Y^{i+1}_A = &\; {} sk^{i+1}_{AB}\oplus R^{i+1}_{A}\\ M^{i+1}_{SA} = &\; {} R^{i+2}_{A} \oplus RN^{i+1}\\ M^{i+1}_{SB} = &\; {} R^{i+2}_{B} \oplus RN^{i+1}\\ H^{i+1}_{SA} = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+2}_{A}\Vert M^{i+1}_{SB}) \\ H^{i+1}_{SB} = &\; {} \mathscr {H}(R^{i+2}_{B}\Vert M^{i+1}_{SB}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CAi+1CBi+1MAi+1=RAi+1⊕RNi+1MBi+1=RBi+1⊕RNi+1TAB′i+1=skABi+1⊕RNi+1HAi+1=H(RAi+1‖MAi+1)HBi+1=H(RBi+1‖MBi+1)YBi+1=skABi+1⊕RBi+1YAi+1=skABi+1⊕RAi+1MSAi+1=RAi+2⊕RNi+1MSBi+1=RBi+2⊕RNi+1HSAi+1=H(RAi+2‖MSBi+1)HSBi+1=H(RBi+2‖MSBi+1)where,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R^{i+2}_{A} = &\; {} PUF^A(C^{i+2}_{A})\\ R^{i+2}_{B} = &\; {} PUF^B(C^{i+2}_{B})\\ C^{i+2}_{A} = &\; {} C^{i+1}_{A}\oplus R^{i+1}_{A}\\ C^{i+2}_{B} = &\; {} C^{i+1}_{B}\oplus R^{i+1}_{B}\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RAi+2=PUFA(CAi+2)RBi+2=PUFB(CBi+2)CAi+2=CAi+1⊕RAi+1CBi+2=CBi+1⊕RBi+1Given those data, the adversary is able to retrieve the following information:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R^i_{A} = &\; {} C^i_{A}\oplus C^{i+1}_{A}\\ RN^i = &\; {} R^i_{A}\oplus M^i_A\\ R^i_{B} = &\; {} M^i_B \oplus RN^i\\ sk^i_{AB} = &\; {} Y^i_{AB}\oplus RN^i \\ R^{i+1}_{A} = &\; {} M^i_{SA} \oplus RN^i\\ R^{i+1}_{B} = &\; {} M^i_{SB} \oplus RN^i\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RAi=CAi⊕CAi+1RNi=RAi⊕MAiRBi=MBi⊕RNiskABi=YABi⊕RNiRAi+1=MSAi⊕RNiRBi+1=MSBi⊕RNiAs mentioned earlier, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$sk^i_{AB}$$\end{document}skABi is the session key that was used during the i th session, and the adversary has access to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+1}_{A}$$\end{document}RAi+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+1}_{B}$$\end{document}RBi+1 also. With this information, the adversary could potentially compromise the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+1){th}$$\end{document}(i+1)th session, as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^{i+1}_{A}{} & {} \\ RN^{i+1} = &\; {} R^{i+1}_{A}\oplus M^{i+1}_A\\ sk^{i+1}_{AB} = &\; {} T'^{i+1}_{AB}\oplus RN^{i+1} \\ R^{i+2}_{A} = &\; {} M^{i+1}_{SA}\oplus RN^{i+1}\\ R^{i+2}_{B} = &\; {} M^{i+1}_{SB} \oplus RN^{i+1}\\ C^{i+2}_{A} = &\; {} C^{i+1}_{A}\oplus R^{i+1}_{A}\\ C^{i+2}_{B} = &\; {} C^{i+1}_{B}\oplus R^{i+1}_{B}\\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CAi+1RNi+1=RAi+1⊕MAi+1skABi+1=TAB′i+1⊕RNi+1RAi+2=MSAi+1⊕RNi+1RBi+2=MSBi+1⊕RNi+1CAi+2=CAi+1⊕RAi+1CBi+2=CBi+1⊕RBi+1which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$sk^{i+1}_{AB}$$\end{document}skABi+1 is the session key that has been used during the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i+1{th}$$\end{document}i+1th session. In fact, if the adversary is able to compromise the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+1){th}$$\end{document}(i+1)th session using the techniques described above, then it may be able to use the information obtained from that session to compromise subsequent sessions as well, leading to complete retrieval of the secret parameters or data that are transferred between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB, and the server.

This passive attack is particularly dangerous because it is hard to detect, and the adversary is able to retrieve sensitive information without actively participating in the communication process. To prevent such attacks, it is crucial to use strong encryption techniques to protect the communication channels and authenticate all parties involved using secure and robust methods. Additionally, it may be helpful to periodically update the secret parameters and keys used in the protocol to further enhance the security of the system.

Impersonation attack

If the adversary has access to the secret parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{R^{i+2}_{A};R^{i+2}_{B};C^{i+2}_{A};C^{i+2}_{B}\}$$\end{document}{RAi+2;RBi+2;CAi+2;CBi+2}, it could potentially impersonate the server, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB in future sessions.

With this information, the adversary could compute the updated CRP responses for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_B$$\end{document}CB in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+2){th}$$\end{document}(i+2)th session and impersonate the server to manipulate these responses. This would allow the adversary to gain access to sensitive information or compromise the integrity of the system.

Desynchronization

To desynchronize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA from the server in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+2){th}$$\end{document}(i+2)th session, an attacker can follow these steps: Eavesdrops on two consecutive sessions (sessions i and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i+1$$\end{document}i+1) of the protocol involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, and extracts the secret parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{R^{i+2}_{A};R^{i+2}_{B};C^{i+2}_{A};C^{i+2}_{B}\}$$\end{document}{RAi+2;RBi+2;CAi+2;CBi+2}.In the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+2){th}$$\end{document}(i+2)th session, the attacker can impersonate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and update its CRP responses to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{i+3}_{A}=C^i_{A}\oplus R^i_{A}$$\end{document}CAi+3=CAi⊕RAi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} phase of the authentication process. The attacker can then send \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<M^i_{SA},H^i_{SA}>$$\end{document}<MSAi,HSAi> to the server. It is enough for the attacker to assign a random value to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+3}_{A}$$\end{document}RAi+3 at this stage.The server will accept the received message and update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA’s record of the CRP to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(C^{i+3}_{A};R^{i+3}_{A})$$\end{document}(CAi+3;RAi+3).However, there is only a probability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{-n}$$\end{document}2-n that the random value assigned to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+3}_{A}$$\end{document}RAi+3 by the attacker will match the value obtained from the PUF function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PUF^A(C^{i+3}_{A})$$\end{document}PUFA(CAi+3). Therefore, the success probability of the attack is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-2^{-n}$$\end{document}1-2-n and the complexity of the attack is three consecutive sessions of the protocol.

Eavesdrops on two consecutive sessions (sessions i and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i+1$$\end{document}i+1) of the protocol involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, and extracts the secret parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{R^{i+2}_{A};R^{i+2}_{B};C^{i+2}_{A};C^{i+2}_{B}\}$$\end{document}{RAi+2;RBi+2;CAi+2;CBi+2}.

Eavesdrops on two consecutive sessions (sessions i and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i+1$$\end{document}i+1) of the protocol involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA, and extracts the secret parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{R^{i+2}_{A};R^{i+2}_{B};C^{i+2}_{A};C^{i+2}_{B}\}$$\end{document}{RAi+2;RBi+2;CAi+2;CBi+2}.

In the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i+2){th}$$\end{document}(i+2)th session, the attacker can impersonate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {C}_A$$\end{document}CA and update its CRP responses to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{i+3}_{A}=C^i_{A}\oplus R^i_{A}$$\end{document}CAi+3=CAi⊕RAi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{i+3}_{A}=PUF^A(C^{i+3}_{A})$$\end{document}RAi+3=PUFA(CAi+3) during the update phase of the authentication process.
